People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

A few weeks, maybe. I wasn’t as down as before—eating a bit more, getting through work better—but I still heard my name by the stairwell. And I still felt pretty sure the hallway cameras were… tracking me somehow.\n\nDoctor: What is the longest time you’ve heard the voice or felt watched when you were not depressed at all?\n\nPatient: I guess… several weeks. My mood had lifted some by then, I was eating and getting through work a little better. But I still heard my name near the stairwell, and I still thought the hallway cameras were tracking me.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt depressed most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. It’s been like that for a long time. The depression part started not long after I moved, and it’s been around… maybe a year and a couple months now.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a stretch of at least 2 weeks when you heard the voice or felt watched and you were not depressed at all?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. There were a few weeks where I wasn’t really depressed like before. I was eating more, managing work better. But I still heard my name by the stairwell, and I still felt like the hallway cameras were tracking me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt so unusually high, energized, or irritable that other people thought you were not your normal self?\n\nPatient: Not that I can think of. I mean, I get tense sometimes, but not… high or energized like that.\n\nDoctor: During those depressed periods, did you lose interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… things I used to like don’t really do much now. Fishing on Saturdays, crosswords with coffee… even showing the younger guys how to strip a floor right. I mostly just let it all sit. Doesn’t give me anything back.\n\nDoctor: During that time when your mood had lifted, were you completely free of depression?\n\nPatient: Not completely, no. It was… lighter, I guess. I was eating and getting through work better for a few weeks, but I still had that stairwell thing, hearing my name, and I still thought the hallway cameras were watching me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard the voice or felt watched during a time when your mood felt completely normal?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there was a few weeks where I felt more normal, I guess. Eating better, getting through work. But I still heard my name in the stairwell, and I still felt like the hallway cameras were… keeping track of me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I finally went to the employee assistance program because I could not keep my work routine straight anymore.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Reserved patient.\nA reserved patient may\n1) provide brief, vague, or evasive answers to questions about their symptoms and experiences,\n2) be reluctant to answer questions about personal information, emotions, or sensitive clinical experiences, and\n3) refrain from voluntarily providing information beyond what the doctor specifically asks.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

People say the cameras are just for the front door, but… I don’t know.\n\nDoctor: When you hear that voice or feel watched, are you usually feeling depressed at the same time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. When I was at my lowest, barely eating and feeling real guilty… like I didn’t care if I woke up… that’s when I was hearing him at work too. And I was pretty sure people in the building were watching me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard that voice or felt watched during a time when you were not feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there was a period like that. A few weeks, maybe. I wasn’t as down as before—eating a bit more, getting through work better—but I still heard my name by the stairwell. And I still felt pretty sure the hallway cameras were… tracking me somehow.\n\nDoctor: What is the longest time you’ve heard the voice or felt watched when you were not depressed at all?\n\nPatient: I guess… several weeks. My mood had lifted some by then, I was eating and getting through work a little better. But I still heard my name near the stairwell, and I still thought the hallway cameras were tracking me.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt depressed most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. It’s been like that for a long time. The depression part started not long after I moved, and it’s been around… maybe a year and a couple months now.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a stretch of at least 2 weeks when you heard the voice or felt watched and you were not depressed at all?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. There were a few weeks where I wasn’t really depressed like before. I was eating more, managing work better. But I still heard my name by the stairwell, and I still felt like the hallway cameras were tracking me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt so unusually high, energized, or irritable that other people thought you were not your normal self?\n\nPatient: Not that I can think of. I mean, I get tense sometimes, but not… high or energized like that.\n\nDoctor: During those depressed periods, did you lose interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… things I used to like don’t really do much now. Fishing on Saturdays, crosswords with coffee… even showing the younger guys how to strip a floor right. I mostly just let it all sit. Doesn’t give me anything back.\n\nDoctor: During that time when your mood had lifted, were you completely free of depression?\n\nPatient: Not completely, no. It was… lighter, I guess. I was eating and getting through work better for a few weeks, but I still had that stairwell thing, hearing my name, and I still thought the hallway cameras were watching me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard the voice or felt watched during a time when your mood felt completely normal?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there was a few weeks where I felt more normal, I guess. Eating better, getting through work. But I still heard my name in the stairwell, and I still felt like the hallway cameras were… keeping track of me.\n\nDoctor: About how long in total have you been hearing that voice or feeling watched?\n\nPatient: I guess… close to a year. Maybe around eleven months, all told.\n\nDoctor: Have the voice or the feeling of being watched been going on continuously for at least 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been more than six months. About eleven months now, I think.\n\nDoctor: Since these experiences began, have you been functioning noticeably worse at work or in taking care of yourself than you were before?\n\nPatient: Yeah… worse. My supervisor started writing me up for missed routes and mistakes I kept making cleaning. I wasn’t really keeping up with church or fishing, or checking in on people like I used to. I ended up going to the employee assistance program because I just couldn’t keep my work routine straight anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your speech is hard to follow or that you jump from one idea to another?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a couple people have said that. People say the cameras are just for the front door, but… I don’t know.\n\nDoctor: When you hear that voice or feel watched, are you usually feeling depressed at the same time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. When I was at my lowest, barely eating and feeling real guilty… like I didn’t care if I woke up… that’s when I was hearing him at work too. And I was pretty sure people in the building were watching me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard that voice or felt watched during a time when you were not feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there was a period like that. A few weeks, maybe. I wasn’t as down as before—eating a bit more, getting through work better—but I still heard my name by the stairwell. And I still felt pretty sure the hallway cameras were… tracking me somehow.\n\nDoctor: What is the longest time you’ve heard the voice or felt watched when you were not depressed at all?\n\nPatient: I guess… several weeks. My mood had lifted some by then, I was eating and getting through work a little better. But I still heard my name near the stairwell, and I still thought the hallway cameras were tracking me.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt depressed most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. It’s been like that for a long time. The depression part started not long after I moved, and it’s been around… maybe a year and a couple months now.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a stretch of at least 2 weeks when you heard the voice or felt watched and you were not depressed at all?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. There were a few weeks where I wasn’t really depressed like before. I was eating more, managing work better. But I still heard my name by the stairwell, and I still felt like the hallway cameras were tracking me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt so unusually high, energized, or irritable that other people thought you were not your normal self?\n\nPatient: Not that I can think of. I mean, I get tense sometimes, but not… high or energized like that.\n\nDoctor: During those depressed periods, did you lose interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… things I used to like don’t really do much now. Fishing on Saturdays, crosswords with coffee… even showing the younger guys how to strip a floor right. I mostly just let it all sit. Doesn’t give me anything back.\n\nDoctor: During that time when your mood had lifted, were you completely free of depression?\n\nPatient: Not completely, no. It was… lighter, I guess. I was eating and getting through work better for a few weeks, but I still had that stairwell thing, hearing my name, and I still thought the hallway cameras were watching me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard the voice or felt watched during a time when your mood felt completely normal?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there was a few weeks where I felt more normal, I guess. Eating better, getting through work. But I still heard my name in the stairwell, and I still felt like the hallway cameras were… keeping track of me.\n\nDoctor: About how long in total have you been hearing that voice or feeling watched?\n\nPatient: I guess… close to a year. Maybe around eleven months, all told.\n\nDoctor: Have the voice or the feeling of being watched been going on continuously for at least 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been more than six months. About eleven months now, I think.\n\nDoctor: Since these experiences began, have you been functioning noticeably worse at work or in taking care of yourself than you were before?\n\nPatient: Yeah… worse. My supervisor started writing me up for missed routes and mistakes I kept making cleaning. I wasn’t really keeping up with church or fishing, or checking in on people like I used to. I ended up going to the employee assistance program because I just couldn’t keep my work routine straight anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your speech is hard to follow or that you jump from one idea to another?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a couple people have said that. Like my supervisor asks which floors I finished, and I’ll start answering, but then I’m talking about the old bus route, or the garden plot, or the church basement. I can hear myself doing it sometimes… not getting back to the question.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself talking much less than you used to, even when you want to say more?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so. I don’t talk much anymore. Even when I’ve got something to say, I just kind of go flat and quiet. It’s… not like me.\n\nDoctor: For at least one month, were the voice, the feeling of being watched, and your trouble staying on topic all happening during the same period?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been more than a month. The voices and that feeling like I’m being watched… that’s been going on maybe 11 months now. The trouble staying on topic was around in that same time, yeah.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Eating better, getting through work. About eleven months now, I think.\n\nDoctor: Since these experiences began, have you been functioning noticeably worse at work or in taking care of yourself than you were before?\n\nPatient: Yeah… worse. My supervisor started writing me up for missed routes and mistakes I kept making cleaning. I wasn’t really keeping up with church or fishing, or checking in on people like I used to. I ended up going to the employee assistance program because I just couldn’t keep my work routine straight anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your speech is hard to follow or that you jump from one idea to another?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a couple people have said that. Like my supervisor asks which floors I finished, and I’ll start answering, but then I’m talking about the old bus route, or the garden plot, or the church basement. I can hear myself doing it sometimes… not getting back to the question.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself talking much less than you used to, even when you want to say more?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so. I don’t talk much anymore. Even when I’ve got something to say, I just kind of go flat and quiet. It’s… not like me.\n\nDoctor: For at least one month, were the voice, the feeling of being watched, and your trouble staying on topic all happening during the same period?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been more than a month. The voices and that feeling like I’m being watched… that’s been going on maybe 11 months now. The trouble staying on topic was around in that same time, yeah.\n\nDoctor: Have you been showing less facial expression or emotional reaction than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I was eating and getting through work better for a few weeks, but I still had that stairwell thing, hearing my name, and I still thought the hallway cameras were watching me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard the voice or felt watched during a time when your mood felt completely normal?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there was a few weeks where I felt more normal, I guess. Eating better, getting through work. But I still heard my name in the stairwell, and I still felt like the hallway cameras were… keeping track of me.\n\nDoctor: About how long in total have you been hearing that voice or feeling watched?\n\nPatient: I guess… close to a year. Maybe around eleven months, all told.\n\nDoctor: Have the voice or the feeling of being watched been going on continuously for at least 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been more than six months. About eleven months now, I think.\n\nDoctor: Since these experiences began, have you been functioning noticeably worse at work or in taking care of yourself than you were before?\n\nPatient: Yeah… worse. My supervisor started writing me up for missed routes and mistakes I kept making cleaning. I wasn’t really keeping up with church or fishing, or checking in on people like I used to. I ended up going to the employee assistance program because I just couldn’t keep my work routine straight anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your speech is hard to follow or that you jump from one idea to another?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a couple people have said that. Like my supervisor asks which floors I finished, and I’ll start answering, but then I’m talking about the old bus route, or the garden plot, or the church basement. I can hear myself doing it sometimes… not getting back to the question.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself talking much less than you used to, even when you want to say more?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so. I don’t talk much anymore. Even when I’ve got something to say, I just kind of go flat and quiet. It’s… not like me.\n\nDoctor: For at least one month, were the voice, the feeling of being watched, and your trouble staying on topic all happening during the same period?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been more than a month. The voices and that feeling like I’m being watched… that’s been going on maybe 11 months now. The trouble staying on topic was around in that same time, yeah.\n\nDoctor: Have you been showing less facial expression or emotional reaction than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. People say I look kind of blank. I don’t really react much. I just go quiet, flat… and even starting my cleaning route feels like I’m pushing a loaded cart uphill.\n\nDoctor: During the time since the voice and the feeling of being watched began, was there ever a period when you were not depressed at all?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there was a few weeks where I wasn’t as down. I was eating a little better, getting through work more. But the voice was still there sometimes, like in the stairwell, and I still felt like the hallway cameras were tracking me."
